People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I don’t really reach for those things the way I used to. It’s hard to tell if it’s less interest or just… everything feels muted.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since your father declined?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really been keeping track. My clothes maybe feel a little different some days, but… I don’t know if my weight has actually changed.\n\nDoctor: Do you have memories or images of your father that keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in a clear way. I think about him sometimes, but I wouldn’t say there are images that keep coming back. It’s more just… there.\n\nDoctor: Has your daily life or relationships with others been affected by these feelings about your father?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I guess. Work’s been harder. I’ve missed some deadlines, and I’ve avoided employee meetings I normally would handle.\n\nAnd I stopped going to my library volunteering. I just… haven’t been able to keep myself steady enough to follow through.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about what you could have done differently for your father?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I keep replaying his appointments, wondering if I should’ve pushed harder or done something different.\n\nAnd then I think about the arguments with my relatives, and it just… it turns into me feeling like I was selfish. Like I failed him. And I’m not much use at work either. Sometimes it feels like I deserve whatever comes from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing certain actions over and over, like checking or cleaning, as a way to deal with these guilty feelings about your father?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I don’t do checking or cleaning because of it. Mostly I just get stuck thinking, but not… actions like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain thoughts or mental acts as a way to deal with these guilty feelings about your father?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I do think about it a lot, but I don’t know if it’s like an urge to repeat something on purpose. It just kind of happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because… I can’t get past this feeling that I’m being punished. For letting my father decline. My sister keeps showing me notes from his doctors, saying I did what I could, but it doesn’t change it. It feels like some judgment’s already been made about me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most of the time since your father declined?\n\nPatient: Yes… most days. I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling, like everything’s lost its color. Even at work, or looking at the garden, it just… doesn’t really reach me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened with your father?\n\nPatient: Yes. I keep going back over everything… every appointment, every choice I made, every argument with family. It always ends with me feeling like I was selfish. Like I failed as a daughter… and honestly, like I’m not much use anywhere, even at work. I know that sounds harsh, but it feels like I deserve whatever comes from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable before your father declined?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I don’t think about enjoyment much lately. Things just feel different, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having less interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe a little. I don’t really reach for those things the way I used to. It’s hard to tell if it’s less interest or just… everything feels muted.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since your father declined?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really been keeping track. My clothes maybe feel a little different some days, but… I don’t know if my weight has actually changed.\n\nDoctor: Do you have memories or images of your father that keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in a clear way. I think about him sometimes, but I wouldn’t say there are images that keep coming back. It’s more just… there.\n\nDoctor: Has your daily life or relationships with others been affected by these feelings about your father?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I guess. Work’s been harder. I’ve missed some deadlines, and I’ve avoided employee meetings I normally would handle.\n\nAnd I stopped going to my library volunteering. I just… haven’t been able to keep myself steady enough to follow through.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about what you could have done differently for your father?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I keep replaying his appointments, wondering if I should’ve pushed harder or done something different.\n\nAnd then I think about the arguments with my relatives, and it just… it turns into me feeling like I was selfish. Like I failed him. And I’m not much use at work either. Sometimes it feels like I deserve whatever comes from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing certain actions over and over, like checking or cleaning, as a way to deal with these guilty feelings about your father?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I don’t do checking or cleaning because of it. Mostly I just get stuck thinking, but not… actions like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain thoughts or mental acts as a way to deal with these guilty feelings about your father?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I do think about it a lot, but I don’t know if it’s like an urge to repeat something on purpose. It just kind of happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like these guilty thoughts about your father are forced into your mind and you can't control them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not exactly forced. They come up a lot, and I don’t feel like I have much control once they start, but it’s hard to explain.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these guilty thoughts about your father are unwanted and intrude into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess unwanted is fair. I don’t sit down and decide to think about it. It just comes in—my father’s appointments, things I said to relatives—and then I start feeling like I’m selfish, like I’m failing at everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're mentally replaying these thoughts about your father over and over, even when you try to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling, like everything’s lost its color. Even at work, or looking at the garden, it just… doesn’t really reach me anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened with your father?\n\nPatient: Yes. I keep going back over everything… every appointment, every choice I made, every argument with family. It always ends with me feeling like I was selfish. Like I failed as a daughter… and honestly, like I’m not much use anywhere, even at work. I know that sounds harsh, but it feels like I deserve whatever comes from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you still enjoy activities that you used to find pleasurable before your father declined?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not really sure how to answer that. I don’t think about enjoyment much lately. Things just feel different, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having less interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe a little. I don’t really reach for those things the way I used to. It’s hard to tell if it’s less interest or just… everything feels muted.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight since your father declined?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really been keeping track. My clothes maybe feel a little different some days, but… I don’t know if my weight has actually changed.\n\nDoctor: Do you have memories or images of your father that keep coming back to you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in a clear way. I think about him sometimes, but I wouldn’t say there are images that keep coming back. It’s more just… there.\n\nDoctor: Has your daily life or relationships with others been affected by these feelings about your father?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I guess. Work’s been harder. I’ve missed some deadlines, and I’ve avoided employee meetings I normally would handle.\n\nAnd I stopped going to my library volunteering. I just… haven’t been able to keep myself steady enough to follow through.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about what you could have done differently for your father?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I keep replaying his appointments, wondering if I should’ve pushed harder or done something different.\n\nAnd then I think about the arguments with my relatives, and it just… it turns into me feeling like I was selfish. Like I failed him. And I’m not much use at work either. Sometimes it feels like I deserve whatever comes from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing certain actions over and over, like checking or cleaning, as a way to deal with these guilty feelings about your father?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I don’t do checking or cleaning because of it. Mostly I just get stuck thinking, but not… actions like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain thoughts or mental acts as a way to deal with these guilty feelings about your father?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I do think about it a lot, but I don’t know if it’s like an urge to repeat something on purpose. It just kind of happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like these guilty thoughts about your father are forced into your mind and you can't control them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not exactly forced. They come up a lot, and I don’t feel like I have much control once they start, but it’s hard to explain.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these guilty thoughts about your father are unwanted and intrude into your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes… I guess unwanted is fair. I don’t sit down and decide to think about it. It just comes in—my father’s appointments, things I said to relatives—and then I start feeling like I’m selfish, like I’m failing at everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're mentally replaying these thoughts about your father over and over, even when you try to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I keep going back over every decision about my father’s appointments, and those tense conversations with relatives. Even if I try to focus on work or something else, it comes back, and I end up feeling like I’m just… selfish. Like I’m a bad daughter, and not much use at work either.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop thinking about your guilt over your father's decline, even when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I can’t really stop it. I keep going back over the appointments, what I should’ve done, what I said to my relatives. And then it just turns into… I’m selfish, I’m not doing enough. Like I deserve whatever comes from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your guilt about your father's decline is related to something specific that happened or something you did during that time?\n\nPatient: I guess it’s tied to… all of it. The appointments for my father, whether I handled them right, and those conversations with my relatives. I keep picking through every decision, and then it turns into me feeling like I’m selfish… like I’m failing as a daughter, and at work too. Like I deserve whatever happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do something specific to try to make up for what you think you did wrong regarding your father's decline?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there’s one specific thing. I just keep going over all of it… the appointments, what I said to my relatives. And it ends up feeling like I should somehow pay for it, I guess. Like I was selfish, and I’m not much good to anyone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your feelings about your father's decline are the main thing that's causing your sadness and guilt?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it’s a big part of it. I keep replaying every decision about his appointments, and the things I said to my relatives. It just ends up feeling like I’m a selfish daughter… and not much use at work either. Like I deserve whatever happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your sadness and guilt are specifically about your father, or are they more about yourself and your own worth?\n\nPatient: It’s… both, I guess. It starts with my father — the appointments, and arguments with relatives — but then it turns into something about me. Like I’m selfish, and I’m no good as a daughter… or even at work. Like I deserve whatever comes from it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
